<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> For every fixed integer <i>r</i> ≥ 3, we prove that every relabeling-invariant Boolean property of simple <i>r</i>-uniform hypergraphs on <i>n</i> vertices satisfies $`\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\nolimits _p(f)\le C_r I_p(f)/(\log n)^{r/(r-1)}`$. The constant depends only on <i>r</i>, and the bound holds uniformly for all $`0\lt p\lt 1`$ without a monotonicity assumption. For increasing properties, it gives the corresponding threshold-width bound with exponent $`r/(r-1)`$, proving the hypergraph threshold-width conjecture of Friedgut and Kalai.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every integer $r\ge3$ there is a finite constant $C_r$ such that, for every integer $n\ge r$, every $0<p<1$, and every hypergraph property $f:\{0,1\}^{E_{n,r}}\to\{0,1\}$, $$\label{eq:main} \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_p(f)\le \frac{C_r}{(\log n)^{r/(r-1)}}I_p(f).$$ No monotonicity assumption is required.
  - Explanation: Vertex permutation invariance forces the stated variance–total-influence bound, uniformly in bias and without monotonicity.
- **C2** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-low)): Let $m\ge2$ be real and let $h:\{0,1\}^J\to\{0,1\}$ satisfy $I_i(h)\le I(h)/m$ for every $i\in J$. With $k=\sigma\log m/64$, one has $$\label{eq:low} \sum_{1\le \|S\|\le k}\widehat h(S)^2\le m^{-1/4}I(h).$$ The sum is empty when $k<1$.
  - Explanation: Small individual influences force a bound on the low-degree Fourier mass.
- **C3** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-block)): Let $r+1\le m\le n$ be integers and set $k=\sigma\log m/64$. For $B$ chosen uniformly among the $m$-subsets of $[n]$, $$\label{eq:block} \sum_{S\subseteq E} \Pr_B(1\le\|S\cap F_B\|\le k)\widehat f(S)^2 \le m^{-1/4}\frac{rm}{n}I_p(f).$$
  - Explanation: A random vertex block preserves enough symmetry to bound averaged restricted Fourier mass.
- **C4** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-capture)): Let $n\ge r\ge3$ and $1\le m\le n$ be integers, let $k>0$, and put $$\alpha=\frac r{r-1},\qquad L=(k/r)^\alpha.$$ Suppose $$\label{eq:capture-condition} rL\frac{m-1}{n-1}\le\frac12.$$ For a uniform $m$-subset $B$ of $[n]$ and every $S\subseteq E_{n,r}$ with $1\le\|S\|\le L$, $$\label{eq:capture} \Pr_B(1\le\|S\cap F_B\|\le k)\ge\frac{m}{2n}.$$
  - Explanation: A support with sufficiently few edges has a low-degree vertex whose isolated capture supplies the probability lower bound.
- **C5** (bound, [source](#stmt-cor-threshold)): For a nontrivial increasing property of $r$-uniform hypergraphs on $n\ge r$ vertices, where $r\ge3$, and every $0<\varepsilon<1/2$, $$p_{1-\varepsilon}-p_\varepsilon \le\frac{2C_r}{(\log n)^{r/(r-1)}} \log\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}.$$
  - Explanation: Increasing nontrivial properties have the stated threshold-width upper bound after integration of the variance inequality.

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Vertex and edge coordinate sets** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Let $r\ge3$ and $n\ge r$ be integers, write $[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}$, and put $E_{n,r}=\binom{[n]}r$.
- **Hypergraph property** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A *hypergraph property* is a Boolean function on this space that is invariant under every permutation of $[n]$.
- **Bernoulli product law** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For $0<p<1$, give the coordinates the independent Bernoulli$(p)$ law, and denote expectation and variance by $\mathbb E_p$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_p$.
- **Pivotal and total influence** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Define the *pivotal influence* of $e$ and the *total influence* of $f$ by $$I_{e,p}(f)=\Pr_p\bigl(f(x_{e\leftarrow1})\ne f(x_{e\leftarrow0})\bigr), \qquad I_p(f)=\sum_{e\in E_{n,r}}I_{e,p}(f).$$
- **Increasing and nontrivial properties** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A property is *increasing* if changing coordinates from $0$ to $1$ cannot decrease its value, and *nontrivial* if it is not constant.
- **Probability quantiles** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For a nontrivial increasing property, put $q(p)=\mathbb E_p f$ and define $p_a=\inf\{p\in[0,1]:q(p)\ge a\}$ for $0<a<1$.
- **Threshold width** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For fixed $0<\varepsilon<1/2$, its *threshold width* is $p_{1-\varepsilon}-p_\varepsilon$.
- **Biased Fourier characters** ([source](#sec:fourier)): For $i\in J$ and $A\subseteq J$, define $$\chi_i(x)=\frac{x_i-p}{\sigma},\qquad \chi_A(x)=\prod_{i\in A}\chi_i(x),\qquad \chi_\varnothing=1.$$
- **Fourier coefficient** ([source](#sec:fourier)): Write $\widehat h(A)=\mathbb E_p[h\chi_A]$.
- **Free edge set** ([source](#sec:restriction)): For a vertex set $B\subseteq[n]$, write $$F_B=\{e\in E:e\cap B\ne\varnothing\}$$ for the edge coordinates that remain free.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_p(f) | ≤ | $\frac{C_r}{(\log n)^{r/(r-1)}}I_p(f)$ | Fixed r; every n at least r and every interior bias. |
| Q2 | k | = | $\sigma\log m/64$ | Real m at least two; sigma is sqrt(p(1-p)). |
| Q3 | \sum_{1\le \|S\|\le k}\widehat h(S)^2 | ≤ | $m^{-1/4}I(h)$ | Individual influences bounded by I(h)/m; sum empty when k is below one. |
| Q4 | \rho | = | $\sigma/4$ | Interior Bernoulli bias; sigma in (0,1/2]. |
| Q5 | \alpha | = | $\frac r{r-1}$ | Integer r at least three. |
| Q6 | L | = | $(k/r)^\alpha$ | Positive k and alpha=r/(r-1). |
| Q7 | \Pr_B(1\le\|S\cap F_B\|\le k) | ≥ | $\frac{m}{2n}$ | Nonempty supports up to L; capture condition required. |
| Q8 | m | = | $\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor$ | n at least the paper's unspecified N_r. |
| Q9 | C_r | = | $\max\left\{2r+(192r)^\alpha,\, \frac{(\log N_r)^\alpha}{4}\right\}.$ | N_r must satisfy every displayed large-n condition; numeric N_r absent. |
| Q10 | p_{1-\varepsilon}-p_\varepsilon | ≤ | $\frac{2C_r}{(\log n)^{r/(r-1)}} \log\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}.$ | Nontrivial increasing invariant properties; epsilon in (0,1/2). |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-lem-low) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-low): Biased Fourier expansion, two-to-four norm estimate and small-influence split. -> C2
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-lem-block) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-block): Condition outside a vertex block, use orbit sizes and average Fourier coefficients and influences. -> C3
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-lem-capture) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-capture): Choose a support vertex of low positive degree and use a conditional union bound. -> C4
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-capture-2): Combine capture and restriction estimates with weighted Parseval; split large and small vertex counts. -> C1
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-cor-threshold) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-threshold): Use monotonicity in the Margulis–Russo identity and integrate the log-odds derivative. -> C5

          "exact_quote": "For a nontrivial increasing property of $r$-uniform hypergraphs on $n\\ge r$ vertices, where $r\\ge3$, and every $0<\\varepsilon<1/2$, $$p_{1-\\varepsilon}-p_\\varepsilon\n \\le\\frac{2C_r}{(\\log n)^{r/(r-1)}}\n                  \\log\\frac{1-\\varepsilon}{\\varepsilon}.$$",

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I-symmetry**: The variance bound requires a Boolean hypergraph property invariant under every vertex permutation; it does not require monotonicity.
- **I-influence**: Pivotal influence is measured over the other coordinates and excludes an additional p(1-p) factor.
- **I-capture**: A support vertex can be chosen before B is sampled with positive degree at most r*s^((r-1)/r); the singleton-intersection event retains exactly its incident support edges.
- **I-threshold**: The threshold-width corollary additionally requires the property to be increasing and nontrivial.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A-coordinate-normalization**: Use variance-scaled pivotal probabilities as the total influence in the stated inequality. -> Changes the normalization by a bias-dependent factor and invalidates direct substitution.
- **A-nonmonotone-threshold**: Apply the threshold-width corollary to a nonmonotone invariant Boolean function. -> The proof identification q prime = total pivotal influence and the unique quantile argument do not follow.
- **A-unchecked-capture**: Claim the m/(2n) singleton-capture probability for an arbitrary block size. -> The avoidance probability need not meet the union-bound lower bound.
- **A-finite-certificate**: Treat the finite witness self-test as certification of Theorem 1 for all vertex counts and biases. -> The finite test does not check Fourier estimates, bias cancellation, or existence of N_r across unbounded n.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The proof of Lemma 4 explicitly chooses a positive-degree vertex before sampling B. Implement that finite witness and its singleton-intersection event, using the proof-specific sufficient condition 2(v-1)(m-1)<=n-1. This does not implement the full asymptotic theorem.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON array of cases {n,r,m,edges}, vertices numbered 0 through n-1.
# PRECONDITIONS: 3 <= r <= n <= 10; 1 <= m <= n; nonempty distinct simple r-edges; 2(v-1)(m-1) <= n-1.
# OUTPUTS: For each case, vertex u, degree d, and exact probability [numerator,denominator] of B intersect V = {u}; invalid cases return an error object.
# TIME: Per case O(r*s + n) arithmetic operations, excluding JSON parsing and integer bit complexity.
# SPACE: Per case O(r*s + n), excluding JSON input/output buffers.
# ARITHMETIC: Arbitrary precision integers and python-flint fmpq; no floating point.
# FAILURE: Invalid input case returns {"error":"precondition"}; malformed JSON raises an error; no silent default.
import json, sys
from math import comb
from flint import fmpq

def construct(c):
    try:
        n, r, m, edges = c['n'], c['r'], c['m'], c['edges']
        assert all(type(z) is int for z in (n, r, m))
        assert 3 <= r <= n <= 10 and 1 <= m <= n
        assert type(edges) is list and edges
        canonical = []
        for e in edges:
            assert type(e) is list and len(e) == r
            assert all(type(a) is int and 0 <= a < n for a in e)
            assert len(set(e)) == r
            canonical.append(tuple(sorted(e)))
        assert len(set(canonical)) == len(canonical)
        degrees = {}
        for e in canonical:
            for a in e:
                degrees[a] = degrees.get(a, 0) + 1
        v = len(degrees)
        assert 2 * (v - 1) * (m - 1) <= n - 1
        u = min(degrees, key=lambda a: (degrees[a], a))
        d = degrees[u]
        ways = comb(n - v, m - 1) if m - 1 <= n - v else 0
        prob = fmpq(ways, comb(n, m))
        return {'u': u, 'd': d, 'prob': [int(prob.numerator), int(prob.denominator)]}
    except (AssertionError, KeyError, TypeError, ValueError):
        return {'error': 'precondition'}

print(json.dumps([construct(c) for c in json.load(sys.stdin)], sort_keys=True))
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: no software architecture, API, implementation or software-performance result is supplied; the object is a permutation-invariant Boolean hypergraph property. |
| games_simulation | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | Inferred adaptation: sensitivity diagnostics for simulations whose state is a simple fixed-uniformity hypergraph with independent equal-bias edge sampling and a vertex-relabeling-invariant Boolean event. The paper supplies an analytic constraint, not a simulation accuracy or sample-complexity guarantee. |
| tools | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | Inferred adaptation: a mathematical diagnostic tool could check that a claimed threshold-width application satisfies Corollary 5. The width estimate concerns nontrivial increasing invariant properties; a concrete numerical constant requires a justified cutoff choice. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | implementable | stated | Lemma 4 constructively selects a positive-degree support vertex before sampling the block. Degree counting and a minimum-degree scan implement this finite step; they do not compute the Fourier expansion or prove the global theorem by testing. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: edge sets and coordinate sets are mathematical representations; no data-structure design, storage bound or operations contract is established. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: the analysis supplies no hardware mapping, accelerator kernel, throughput claim or resource model. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: the paper treats Boolean hypergraph properties under a product law, with no adversary model, cryptographic construction or security reduction. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: no learning task, hypothesis class, training procedure or generalisation guarantee is defined. Boolean-function Fourier analysis alone does not establish a learning contract. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: the support-degree choice is a proof witness; there is no optimisation objective, feasible-program contract, approximation ratio or solver result. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: vertex permutation symmetry is an invariance assumption on a mathematical event, with no network protocol, communication model or fault semantics. |
| other (probabilistic sensitivity analysis) | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Inferred adaptation to probabilistic sensitivity analysis: Theorem 1 constrains variance by total pivotal influence for invariant hypergraph events, including nonmonotone events. This is an analytic contract requiring verification of the model assumptions. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L1** (hypothesis): The main result concerns Boolean properties of simple uniform hypergraphs invariant under every vertex permutation. Blocked use: ['Applying the theorem to weighted, directed, nonuniform or nonsymmetric hypergraphs without an additional argument.']
- **L2** (hypothesis): The theorem fixes an integer uniformity at least three, counts vertices, and requires at least as many vertices as the uniformity. Its constant may depend on the uniformity. Blocked use: ['A uniform constant for growing uniformity.', 'Substituting edge count for vertex count in the displayed bound.', 'Claiming the graph case follows from this theorem.']
- **L3** (hypothesis): The measure has independent coordinates with one common Bernoulli bias strictly between zero and one. Blocked use: ['Correlated-edge distributions.', 'Unequal coordinate biases.', 'Direct use at endpoint biases without a limiting argument.']
- **L4** (hypothesis): Influence is pivotal disagreement probability over the other coordinates; it carries no additional bias-variance factor. Blocked use: ['Replacing pivotal influence with a normalization that multiplies it by the bias variance while retaining the same bound.']
- **L5** (hypothesis): The variance bound allows nonmonotone properties, but the threshold-width corollary requires an increasing nonconstant property and an interior tolerance. Blocked use: ['Using the threshold-width corollary for a nonmonotone or constant property.']
- **L6** (edge_case): The low-degree estimate requires every individual influence to be at most total influence divided by the block parameter; below unit cutoff its sum is empty. Blocked use: ['Claiming nonempty low-degree information when the cutoff is below one.', 'Applying the low-degree lemma without its individual-influence hypothesis.']
- **L7** (hypothesis): The averaged restriction lemma uses an integer block size strictly larger than uniformity and at most vertex count. Blocked use: ['Using its orbit-size argument with a block no larger than uniformity.', 'Fixing edges meeting the block instead of edges wholly outside it.']
- **L8** (hypothesis): The capture probability bound requires the support-size cutoff and the displayed union-bound condition. Blocked use: ['Transferring the capture probability guarantee to arbitrary support sizes.', 'Dropping the block-size condition.']
- **L9** (counterexample_scope): Single-vertex capture can retain several support edges. The selected vertex depends on the support and is chosen before sampling the block. Blocked use: ['Treating capture as retaining exactly one edge.', 'Using one fixed vertex simultaneously for every Fourier support without proof.']
- **L10** (nonconstructive): The completion establishes existence of a sufficiently large cutoff but does not supply a numerical cutoff for each uniformity. Consequently the displayed all-size constant is not a ready numerical calibration. Blocked use: ['Reporting a numerical all-size constant without separately validating a cutoff.', 'Claiming the source provides an executable optimal constant-selection algorithm.']
- **L11** (formal_scope): An exact finite implementation of the support-vertex selection checks a combinatorial construction; it does not certify the analytic theorem for every Boolean hypergraph property. Blocked use: ['Treating small-support enumeration as a proof of the full variance or threshold-width bound.']
- **L12** (edge_case): An empty captured low-degree sum is consistent with the proof; the high-degree tail can bound the entire variance. Blocked use: ['Treating an empty low-degree sum as a failure of the theorem.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


For every fixed integer <i>r</i> ≥ 3, we prove that every relabeling-invariant Boolean property of simple <i>r</i>-uniform hypergraphs on <i>n</i> vertices satisfies $`\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\nolimits _p(f)\le C_r I_p(f)/(\log n)^{r/(r-1)}`$. The constant depends only on <i>r</i>, and the bound holds uniformly for all $`0\lt p\lt 1`$ without a monotonicity assumption. For increasing properties, it gives the corresponding threshold-width bound with exponent $`r/(r-1)`$, proving the hypergraph threshold-width conjecture of Friedgut and Kalai.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>
#### Statements index

| anchor | kind | label | number | first sentence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| [stmt-thm-main](#stmt-thm-main) | theorem | `thm:main` | 1 | For every integer $r\ge3$ there is a finite constant $C_r$ such that, for every integer $n\ge r$, every $0<p<1$, and every hypergraph property $f:\{0,1\}^{E_{n,r}}\to\{0,1\}$, |
| [stmt-lem-low](#stmt-lem-low) | lemma | `lem:low` | 2 | Let $m\ge2$ be real and let $h:\{0,1\}^J\to\{0,1\}$ satisfy $I_i(h)\le I(h)/m$ for every $i\in J$. |
| [stmt-lem-block](#stmt-lem-block) | lemma | `lem:block` | 3 | Let $r+1\le m\le n$ be integers and set $k=\sigma\log m/64$. |
| [stmt-lem-capture](#stmt-lem-capture) | lemma | `lem:capture` | 4 | Let $n\ge r\ge3$ and $1\le m\le n$ be integers, let $k>0$, and put $$\alpha=\frac r{r-1},\qquad L=(k/r)^\alpha.$$ Suppose |
| [stmt-cor-threshold](#stmt-cor-threshold) | corollary | `cor:threshold` | 5 | For a nontrivial increasing property of $r$-uniform hypergraphs on $n\ge r$ vertices, where $r\ge3$, and every $0<\varepsilon<1/2$, |

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Let $r\ge3$ and $n\ge r$ be integers, write $[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}$, and put $E_{n,r}=\binom{[n]}r$. An element of $\{0,1\}^{E_{n,r}}$ represents a simple $r$-uniform hypergraph. A *hypergraph property* is a Boolean function on this space that is invariant under every permutation of $[n]$. For $0<p<1$, give the coordinates the independent Bernoulli$(p)$ law, and denote expectation and variance by $\mathbb E_p$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_p$.

For an edge $e$, let $x_{e\leftarrow b}$ be $x$ with its $e$-coordinate set to $b$. Define the *pivotal influence* of $e$ and the *total influence* of $f$ by $$I_{e,p}(f)=\Pr_p\bigl(f(x_{e\leftarrow1})\ne
                         f(x_{e\leftarrow0})\bigr),
 \qquad I_p(f)=\sum_{e\in E_{n,r}}I_{e,p}(f).$$ The probability in the first expression is over the other coordinates; it does not include an additional factor of $p(1-p)$. All logarithms are natural, and $n$ always counts vertices.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:main"></a>
**Theorem 1**. *For every integer $r\ge3$ there is a finite constant $C_r$ such that, for every integer $n\ge r$, every $0<p<1$, and every hypergraph property $f:\{0,1\}^{E_{n,r}}\to\{0,1\}$, $$\label{eq:main}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_p(f)\le \frac{C_r}{(\log n)^{r/(r-1)}}I_p(f).$$ No monotonicity assumption is required.*

</div>

The theorem measures how vertex symmetry forces sensitivity to edge changes. If the probability of a property stays bounded away from zero and one, its total influence must grow at least as $(\log n)^{r/(r-1)}$.

A property is *increasing* if changing coordinates from $0$ to $1$ cannot decrease its value, and *nontrivial* if it is not constant. For a nontrivial increasing property, put $q(p)=\mathbb E_p f$ and define $p_a=\inf\{p\in[0,1]:q(p)\ge a\}$ for $0<a<1$. For fixed $0<\varepsilon<1/2$, its *threshold width* is $p_{1-\varepsilon}-p_\varepsilon$. Friedgut and Kalai conjectured that this width is at most $O_{r,\varepsilon}((\log n)^{-r/(r-1)})$ for every increasing $r$-uniform hypergraph property <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[FK96](#ref-FK96), Section 5, p. 2999]. Their formulation counts edge coordinates rather than vertices; for fixed $r$, $\log\binom nr\sim r\log n$ as $n\to\infty$. Corollary <a href="#cor:threshold" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:threshold">5</a> proves this conjectured bound by integrating <a href="#eq:main" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main">[eq:main]</a>, with an explicit dependence on $\varepsilon$.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
###### Context and attribution.

Kahn, Kalai, and Linial <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[KKL88](#ref-KKL88)] established a fundamental link between Fourier analysis and the influences of Boolean functions. For a function invariant under a transitive group on $M$ coordinates, their theorem gives a total-influence lower bound of order $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_{1/2}(f)\log M$ at the uniform measure. Bourgain, Kahn, Kalai, Katznelson, and Linial <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[BKKKL92](#ref-BKKKL92)] extended the influence method to product spaces. Friedgut and Kalai <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[FK96](#ref-FK96)] used influence inequalities to prove sharp thresholds for increasing families with transitive symmetry. For hypergraph properties, the induced action on the $\binom nr$ edge coordinates is transitive. The richer structure of vertex permutations, however, contains more information than this single transitivity statement.

Bourgain and Kalai <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[BK97](#ref-BK97), Corollary 4.1] studied how group actions on sets of coordinates improve influence estimates. For fixed $r$ and every $\eta>0$, their result gives a lower bound of order $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_{1/2}(f)(\log n)^{r/(r-1)-\eta}$ for the total influence of a hypergraph property at the uniform measure. Their influence results already allow nonmonotone functions. Kelman, Kindler, Lifshitz, Minzer, and Safra obtained further estimates for group-invariant functions <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[KKLMS20](#ref-KKLMS20)]. For graph properties, these give $I_{1/2}(f)\ge c\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_{1/2}(f)(\log n)^2/(\log\log n)^2$ for sufficiently large $n$; see the arXiv version cited in <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[KKLMS20](#ref-KKLMS20), Section 7.4]. The present proof uses vertex symmetry through restrictions: all edges touching a vertex block remain free, and only edges wholly outside the block are fixed. This is the method of the companion manuscript *A Sharp Threshold Bound for Monotone Graph Properties <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Graph2026](#ref-Graph2026), Theorem 1.2 and Sections 2–3]* , which establishes the corresponding variance bound with denominator $(\log n)^2$ for graph properties, uniformly in $p$ and without monotonicity. We reproduce its Fourier estimate and restriction argument in full. The additional combinatorial step replaces the graph degree estimate by its $r$-uniform counterpart, producing the exponent $r/(r-1)$.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
###### Proof strategy.

Put $\sigma=\sqrt{p(1-p)}$ and $\alpha=r/(r-1)$. The Fourier expansion for the Bernoulli product measure is indexed by sets $S$ of hyperedges. Its squared nonconstant coefficients sum to the variance; weighting them by $|S|$ gives $\sigma^2I_p(f)$. Thus the influence controls high degrees. The task is to bound the remaining mass using symmetry.

For large $n$, choose a uniformly random block $B$ of $m$ vertices, with $m$ about $\sqrt n$, and leave free all edges meeting $B$. Every permutation within $B$ survives an assignment to the other coordinates, and every free-edge orbit has at least $m$ elements. Section <a href="#sec:fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:fourier">2</a> bounds the restricted Fourier mass up to a degree $k$ proportional to $\sigma\log m$ by $m^{-1/4}$ times the restricted total influence. Its linear dependence on influence allows averaging over the assigned coordinates.

Regard a nonempty Fourier index $S$ as an $r$-uniform hypergraph with $s$ edges. It has a vertex of positive degree at most $r s^{(r-1)/r}$. For $s\le L=(k/r)^\alpha$, this degree is at most $k$. The event that $B$ meets the union of these edges only at that vertex has probability comparable to $m/n$ and leaves between one and $k$ support edges free. After averaging over the assignments and $B$, the restricted total influence is at most $(rm/n)I_p(f)$, so the two factors of $m/n$ cancel. Section <a href="#sec:restriction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:restriction">3</a> thereby controls the original Fourier mass up to degree $L$.

Since $L$ is of order $(\sigma\log n)^\alpha$, the remaining mass is at most $\sigma^2I_p(f)/L$, with coefficient of order $\sigma^{2-\alpha}(\log n)^{-\alpha}$. The positive exponent $2-\alpha$ makes this bound uniform in $p$. Section <a href="#sec:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:completion">4</a> completes the estimate and derives the threshold consequence.

<a id="sec:fourier"></a>
### A Fourier estimate for small individual influences

We first work on an arbitrary finite coordinate set $J$. Our objective is to control low-degree Fourier mass when every individual influence is small relative to the total. We follow the elementary argument in <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Graph2026](#ref-Graph2026), Lemma 2.1]; its analytic ingredient is a two-to-four norm estimate of the type underlying the classical hypercontractive inequalities <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Bonami70](#ref-Bonami70), [Beckner75](#ref-Beckner75)]. The proof below includes the dependence on the bias.

Fix $0<p<1$ and put $\sigma=\sqrt{p(1-p)}$. For $i\in J$ and $A\subseteq J$, define $$\chi_i(x)=\frac{x_i-p}{\sigma},\qquad
 \chi_A(x)=\prod_{i\in A}\chi_i(x),\qquad \chi_\varnothing=1.$$ Independence, zero means, and unit second moments show that the $\chi_A$ form an orthonormal basis. Write $\widehat h(A)=\mathbb E_p[h\chi_A]$. Throughout this section, norms and expectations use this product law, and $I_i(h)$ and $I(h)$ denote pivotal and total influence on $J$.

<a id="eq:parseval"></a>
For Boolean $h$, let $\Delta_i h$ be the function on the other coordinates obtained by subtracting the section at $x_i=0$ from the section at $x_i=1$. Direct expectation in coordinate $i$ gives $$\widehat h(A\cup\{i\})=
 \sigma\widehat{\Delta_i h}(A)\qquad(A\subseteq J\setminus\{i\}).$$ Because $\Delta_i h\in\{-1,0,1\}$, Parseval’s identity yields $$\label{eq:parseval}
 \sum_{S\subseteq J}|S|\widehat h(S)^2=\sigma^2 I(h),
 \qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_p(h)=\sum_{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq J}\widehat h(S)^2.$$ In particular, this identity uses no sign condition on $\Delta_i h$.

<a id="stmt-lem-low"></a>
<div id="lem:low" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:low"></a>
**Lemma 2** (Low-degree mass). *Let $m\ge2$ be real and let $h:\{0,1\}^J\to\{0,1\}$ satisfy $I_i(h)\le I(h)/m$ for every $i\in J$. With $k=\sigma\log m/64$, one has $$\label{eq:low}
 \sum_{1\le |S|\le k}\widehat h(S)^2\le m^{-1/4}I(h).$$ The sum is empty when $k<1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-low"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-low</summary>

<a id="eq:24"></a>
*Proof.* We will use a two-to-four norm estimate to bound low-degree projections of the differences $\Delta_i h$. Set $\rho=\sigma/4$ and define the linear operator $T$ by $T\chi_A=\rho^{|A|}\chi_A$. We first prove $$\label{eq:24}
 \|Tg\|_4\le\|g\|_2$$ for every real-valued function $g$ on the product space. For one coordinate, $|\chi_i|\le1/\sigma$ and $\mathbb E\chi_i^2=1$ imply $|\mathbb E\chi_i^3|\le1/\sigma$ and $\mathbb E\chi_i^4\le1/\sigma^2$. For real $a,b$, expansion and $2|ab^3|\le a^2b^2+b^4$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E(a+\rho b\chi_i)^4
 &\le a^4+6\rho^2a^2b^2+
       \frac{2\rho^3}{\sigma}(a^2b^2+b^4)
       +\frac{\rho^4}{\sigma^2}b^4\\
 &=a^4+\frac{13\sigma^2}{32}a^2b^2
       +\frac{9\sigma^2}{256}b^4
 \le(a^2+b^2)^2,
\end{aligned}$$ where the last inequality uses $\sigma\le1/2$.

To extend this estimate to the product, induct on $|J|$, starting with the constant functions when $J$ is empty. Write $g=a+b\chi_i$, where $a,b$ depend on the remaining coordinates, and use $T$ for the operator on those coordinates as well. The one-coordinate estimate, applied pointwise, followed by the $L^2$ triangle inequality gives $$\|Tg\|_4^2\le\|(Ta)^2+(Tb)^2\|_2
 \le\|Ta\|_4^2+\|Tb\|_4^2
 \le\|a\|_2^2+\|b\|_2^2=\|g\|_2^2.$$ This proves <a href="#eq:24" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:24">[eq:24]</a>.

Let $P_{\le k}$ be the orthogonal projection onto the span of $\chi_A$ with $|A|\le k$. For every $u$ in this span, $$\|u\|_4\le\|T^{-1}u\|_2\le\rho^{-k}\|u\|_2.$$ Taking the supremum of $|\mathbb E[gu]|$ over unit $L^2$ vectors in this span, and applying Hölder’s inequality, therefore gives <a id="eq:projection"></a>$$\label{eq:projection}
 \|P_{\le k}g\|_2\le\rho^{-k}\|g\|_{4/3}.$$ These inequalities are valid for real $k\ge0$, with no rounding required.

<a id="eq:derivative-mass"></a>
Each nonempty Fourier support is counted at least once when summed over its coordinates. Using the difference identity and enlarging the resulting degree cutoff from $k-1$ to $k$, we obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{1\le|S|\le k}\widehat h(S)^2
 &\le\sigma^2\sum_{i\in J}
       \|P_{\le k}\Delta_i h\|_2^2 \notag\\
 &\le\sigma^2\rho^{-2k}\sum_{i\in J}I_i(h)^{3/2}.
 \label{eq:derivative-mass}
\end{aligned}$$ Here each projection acts on the coordinates other than $i$, and $\|\Delta_i h\|_{4/3}^2=I_i(h)^{3/2}$ because $|\Delta_i h|$ is an indicator.

The function $\sigma\log(4/\sigma)$ is increasing on $(0,1/2]$ and is at most $2$. Hence $\rho^{-2k}\le m^{1/16}$. If $I(h)\le m^{1/4}$, then $I_i(h)^{1/2}\le m^{-3/8}$, so <a href="#eq:derivative-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:derivative-mass">[eq:derivative-mass]</a> is at most $\sigma^2m^{-5/16}I(h)\le m^{-1/4}I(h)$. If $I(h)>m^{1/4}$, the right side of <a href="#eq:low" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:low">[eq:low]</a> exceeds $1$, whereas its left side is at most $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_p(h)\le1/4$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:restriction"></a>
### Restriction to a vertex block

Return to a hypergraph property $f$ on $E=E_{n,r}$ and fix the bias $p$, with $\sigma=\sqrt{p(1-p)}$. Its Fourier coefficients use the basis of Section <a href="#sec:fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:fourier">2</a>, with $J=E$. The restriction method of <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Graph2026](#ref-Graph2026), Lemma 3.1 and Proposition 3.2] preserves a large permutation group after conditioning. We first obtain an averaged estimate for restricted Fourier degrees, and then relate those degrees to the sizes of the original supports.

For a vertex set $B\subseteq[n]$, write $$F_B=\{e\in E:e\cap B\ne\varnothing\}$$ for the edge coordinates that remain free.

<a id="stmt-lem-block"></a>
<div id="lem:block" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:block"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Averaged restriction estimate). *Let $r+1\le m\le n$ be integers and set $k=\sigma\log m/64$. For $B$ chosen uniformly among the $m$-subsets of $[n]$, $$\label{eq:block}
 \sum_{S\subseteq E}
 \Pr_B(1\le|S\cap F_B|\le k)\widehat f(S)^2
 \le m^{-1/4}\frac{rm}{n}I_p(f).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-block"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-block</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $B$ and an assignment $y$ to the coordinates in $E\setminus F_B$. Denote the resulting Boolean function on $F_B$ by $f_y$. Every permutation supported on $B$ fixes each edge outside $F_B$ individually, and consequently fixes $y$. Thus $f_y$ is invariant under all these permutations, regardless of the assignment.

An edge with $j$ vertices in $B$ has an orbit of size $\binom mj$: its outside part is fixed and its inside part can be any $j$-subset of $B$. Since $1\le j\le r<m$, this size is at least $m$. The free product measure is invariant under the same permutations, so influences are equal within each orbit. It follows that $$I_e(f_y)\le I(f_y)/m\qquad(e\in F_B).$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:low" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:low">2</a> therefore applies to every $f_y$.

Average its conclusion over the product law of $y$. For $A\subseteq F_B$, the Fourier expansion of $f$ gives $$\widehat{f_y}(A)=
 \sum_{D\subseteq E\setminus F_B}\widehat f(A\cup D)\chi_D(y).$$ Orthonormality in the assigned coordinates yields $$\mathbb E_y\bigl[\widehat{f_y}(A)^2\bigr]
 =\sum_{D\subseteq E\setminus F_B}\widehat f(A\cup D)^2.$$ Also $\mathbb E_y I_e(f_y)=I_{e,p}(f)$ for every $e\in F_B$, by conditioning the disagreement event. Consequently $$\sum_{S:\,1\le|S\cap F_B|\le k}\widehat f(S)^2
 \le m^{-1/4}\sum_{e\in F_B}I_{e,p}(f).$$ Finally average over $B$. For each fixed edge $e$, a union bound on its $r$ vertices gives $\Pr_B(e\in F_B)\le rm/n$, proving <a href="#eq:block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block">[eq:block]</a>. ◻

</details>

The next lemma extends the graph capture argument of <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Graph2026](#ref-Graph2026), Lemma 3.3] to identify the hypergraph supports detected by this estimate. Here a support $S$ is itself a set of distinct $r$-edges; its vertex union contains no isolated vertices.

<a id="stmt-lem-capture"></a>
<div id="lem:capture" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:capture-condition"></a>
<a id="eq:capture"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Capturing a support at one vertex). *Let $n\ge r\ge3$ and $1\le m\le n$ be integers, let $k>0$, and put $$\alpha=\frac r{r-1},\qquad L=(k/r)^\alpha.$$ Suppose $$\label{eq:capture-condition}
 rL\frac{m-1}{n-1}\le\frac12.$$ For a uniform $m$-subset $B$ of $[n]$ and every $S\subseteq E_{n,r}$ with $1\le|S|\le L$, $$\label{eq:capture}
 \Pr_B(1\le|S\cap F_B|\le k)\ge\frac{m}{2n}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-capture"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-capture</summary>

*Proof.* Fix such an $S$, write $s=|S|$, and let $V=\bigcup_{e\in S}e$ and $v=|V|$. Because $s\le\binom vr\le v^r$, the average degree of the hypergraph with edge set $S$ is at most $$\frac{rs}{v}\le r s^{(r-1)/r}\le k.$$ Choose a vertex $u\in V$ of degree $d_S(u)$ between $1$ and $k$. This choice depends only on $S$, before $B$ is sampled.

Conditional on $u\in B$, each other vertex lies in $B$ with probability $(m-1)/(n-1)$. The union bound and $v\le rs\le rL$ therefore give $$\begin{aligned}
 \Pr_B(B\cap V=\{u\})
 &\ge\frac mn\left(1-(v-1)\frac{m-1}{n-1}\right)\\
 &\ge\frac m{2n}.
\end{aligned}$$ On this event, the edges of $S$ meeting $B$ are exactly those containing $u$. Thus $|S\cap F_B|=d_S(u)\in[1,k]$. ◻

</details>

The captured set need not consist of a single edge: the block meets one support vertex, and retains every support edge incident with it. Also the vertex $u$ may vary with $S$, since <a href="#eq:capture" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:capture">[eq:capture]</a> is a separate probability bound for each nonnegative summand in <a href="#eq:block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block">[eq:block]</a>.

<a id="eq:captured-mass"></a>
Combining Lemmas <a href="#lem:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:block">3</a> and <a href="#lem:capture" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:capture">4</a> gives, whenever their hypotheses hold, $$\label{eq:captured-mass}
 \sum_{1\le|S|\le L}\widehat f(S)^2
 \le 2r m^{-1/4}I_p(f).$$ If $L<1$, this conclusion holds because the sum is empty. Otherwise, restrict the nonnegative sum in <a href="#eq:block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block">[eq:block]</a> to $1\le|S|\le L$ and divide by the lower bound $m/(2n)$. This completes the transfer from restricted degree $k$ to original degree $(k/r)^{r/(r-1)}$.

<a id="sec:completion"></a>
### The uniform bound and its threshold consequence

We now choose the block size so that <a href="#eq:captured-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:captured-mass">[eq:captured-mass]</a> controls the low-degree part of the variance. The weighted Parseval identity will control the remaining degrees.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-capture-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-capture</summary>

<a id="eq:large-n"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Fix $r\ge3$ and write $\alpha=r/(r-1)$. Choose an integer $N_r>r$ such that, for every $n\ge N_r$ and $m=\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor$, $$\label{eq:large-n}
 \begin{gathered}
 m\ge r+1,\qquad \log m\ge\tfrac13\log n,\\
 r(\log n)^\alpha n^{-1/2}\le\tfrac12,\qquad
 m^{-1/4}(\log n)^\alpha\le1.
 \end{gathered}$$ Such an $N_r$ exists: $m$ is asymptotic to $\sqrt n$, and any fixed power of $\log n$ grows more slowly than a positive power of $n$. In particular, the choice is independent of $p$ and $f$.

Assume first that $n\ge N_r$, and set $$\sigma=\sqrt{p(1-p)},\qquad
 k=\frac{\sigma\log m}{64},\qquad L=(k/r)^\alpha.$$ Both cutoffs are positive. Since $k/r\le\log n$ and $$\frac{m-1}{n-1}\le\frac{\sqrt n-1}{n-1}
 =\frac1{\sqrt n+1}\le n^{-1/2},$$ the third condition in <a href="#eq:large-n" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-n">[eq:large-n]</a> implies <a href="#eq:capture-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:capture-condition">[eq:capture-condition]</a>. Thus <a href="#eq:captured-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:captured-mass">[eq:captured-mass]</a> and the fourth condition in <a href="#eq:large-n" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-n">[eq:large-n]</a> give <a id="eq:low-final"></a>$$\label{eq:low-final}
 \sum_{1\le|S|\le L}\widehat f(S)^2
 \le\frac{2r}{(\log n)^\alpha}I_p(f).$$

For the remaining degrees, <a href="#eq:parseval" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:parseval">[eq:parseval]</a> gives $$\sum_{|S|>L}\widehat f(S)^2
 \le\frac1L\sum_S|S|\widehat f(S)^2
 =\frac{\sigma^2}{L}I_p(f).$$ The decisive bias dependence is <a id="eq:bias-cancellation"></a>$$\label{eq:bias-cancellation}
 \frac{\sigma^2}{L}
 =\frac{(64r)^\alpha\sigma^{2-\alpha}}{(\log m)^\alpha}
 \le\frac{(192r)^\alpha}{(\log n)^\alpha}.$$ Indeed $2-\alpha=(r-2)/(r-1)>0$, $\sigma\le1$, and $\log m\ge(\log n)/3$. This estimate remains valid at arbitrarily small positive $\sigma$. If $L<1$, the low-degree sum is empty and the same tail estimate bounds the whole variance.

Adding the two parts proves <a href="#eq:main" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main">[eq:main]</a> for $n\ge N_r$ with constant $2r+(192r)^\alpha$. For $r\le n<N_r$, <a href="#eq:parseval" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:parseval">[eq:parseval]</a> gives $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_p(f)\le\sigma^2 I_p(f)\le I_p(f)/4$. Therefore a valid choice for all $n$ and $p$ is $$C_r=\max\left\{2r+(192r)^\alpha,\,
                       \frac{(\log N_r)^\alpha}{4}\right\}.$$ ◻

</details>

We finish with the threshold consequence stated in the introduction. Recall that $q(p)=\mathbb E_p f$ and $p_a=\inf\{p\in[0,1]:q(p)\ge a\}$.

<a id="stmt-cor-threshold"></a>
<div id="cor:threshold" class="corollary">

**Corollary 5**. *For a nontrivial increasing property of $r$-uniform hypergraphs on $n\ge r$ vertices, where $r\ge3$, and every $0<\varepsilon<1/2$, $$p_{1-\varepsilon}-p_\varepsilon
 \le\frac{2C_r}{(\log n)^{r/(r-1)}}
                  \log\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-threshold"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-threshold</summary>

*Proof.* The Margulis–Russo identity relates pivotal influences to the probability derivative; see <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Margulis74](#ref-Margulis74)], <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Russo81](#ref-Russo81), Lemma 3], and <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[FK96](#ref-FK96), Lemma 2.2]. Here it follows directly by giving each coordinate its own bias: the expectation is affine in each bias separately, and its partial derivative in coordinate $e$ is $\mathbb E_p[f(x_{e\leftarrow1})-f(x_{e\leftarrow0})]$. Monotonicity identifies this with $I_{e,p}(f)$. Differentiating along the common bias gives $q'(p)=I_p(f)$.

Nontrivial monotonicity gives $q(0)=0$ and $q(1)=1$. Some pair of configurations differing in one coordinate has different values, so its pivotal event has positive probability for every $0<p<1$. Hence $q'(p)>0$ there, and the stated quantiles exist uniquely. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> implies $$\frac{q'(p)}{q(p)(1-q(p))}
 \ge\frac{(\log n)^{r/(r-1)}}{C_r}.$$ Integrating from $p_\varepsilon$ to $p_{1-\varepsilon}$, using the antiderivative $\log(q/(1-q))$, proves the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
